# ACON: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents

## Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as agents in dynamic real-world environments,
where success depends on maintaining precise records of actions and observations. However, the
resulting unbounded context growth in long-horizon agentic tasks makes two critical bottlenecks:
prohibitive inference memory costs and reasoning degradation due to irrelevant information. Existing
compression methods fail to fully address this, often relying on brittle heuristics or requiring
parameter updates impractical for proprietary or large-scale LLMs. We introduce Agent Context
Optimization (ACON), a unified framework that optimally compresses both observations and history
into concise, informative representations. Distinct from prior works, ACON employs an optimization
in natural language space: it iteratively refines compression guidelines based on failure analysis
of the agent, ensuring critical state information is preserved without model fine-tuning. To further
minimize computational overhead, we distill the optimized compressor into smaller models.
Experiments on AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Multi-objective QA demonstrate that ACON reduces peak
token usage by 26-54% while improving task success over existing compression baselines. Notably, it
enables smaller LMs to function effectively as long-horizon agents, achieving up to 46% performance
improvement by mitigating context distraction. Our code is available at
https://github.com/microsoft/acon.

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

####### Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as agents in dynamic real-world environments,
where success depends on maintaining precise records of actions and observations. However, the
resulting unbounded context growth in long-horizon agentic tasks makes two critical bottlenecks:
prohibitive inference memory costs and reasoning degradation due to irrelevant information. Existing
compression methods fail to fully address this, often relying on brittle heuristics or requiring
parameter updates impractical for proprietary or large-scale LLMs. We introduce
<span id="abstract1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agent Context Optimization</span>
(<span id="abstract1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span>), a unified framework that
optimally compresses both observations and history into concise, informative representations.
Distinct from prior works, <span id="abstract1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span>
employs an optimization in natural language space: it iteratively refines compression guidelines
based on failure analysis of the agent, ensuring critical state information is preserved without
model fine-tuning. To further minimize computational overhead, we distill the optimized compressor
into smaller models. Experiments on AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Multi-objective QA demonstrate that
<span id="abstract1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> reduces peak token usage by
26–54% while improving task success over existing compression baselines. Notably, it enables smaller
LMs to function effectively as long-horizon agents, achieving up to 46% performance improvement by
mitigating context distraction. Our code is available at <a href="https://github.com/microsoft/acon"
class="ltx_ref ltx_href">https://github.com/microsoft/acon</a>.

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Large language models (LLMs) have become the backbone of AI agents, enabling them to plan and act in
dynamic environments (<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">54</a>). However, these tasks often
unfold over extended horizons, requiring the agent to maintain a continuous record of observations,
tool outputs, and evolving states. In such settings, context is not auxiliary but foundational;
losing a single detail such as a file path or an API parameter can derail the entire workflow. As
interactions accumulate, context grows unbounded as shown
in <a href="#S1.F2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure 2</span></a>, making two major bottlenecks. First, the inference
memory cost of transformers scales with context length, resulting long-horizon reasoning
computationally prohibitive due to the massive KV cache
requirements (<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">40</a>). Second, excessively long contexts dilute
relevant information, distracting the model with outdated or extraneous details and degrading
decision quality (<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">33</a>).

</div>

<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S1.F1.5" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>: </span><span id="S1.F1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">Accuracy-Peak tokens trade-off<span id="S1.F1.6.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> on AppWorld benchmark (<a href="#bib.bib34"
class="ltx_ref">39</a>). We compare average accuracy versus peak tokens in history compression.
<span id="S1.F1.6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> (ours) reduces token cost
while preserving accuracy for the large model (gpt-4.1) relative to a naive prompting baseline, and
even improves accuracy on smaller model (Qwen-14B). More results are in <a href="#S4"
class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 4</span></a>.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<figure id="S1.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2510.00615v3/01_concept_5.png" id="S1.F2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="476" height="171"
alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S1.F2.9" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">Figure 2</span>: </span><span id="S1.F2.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">Motivation: Unbounded context in LLM agents.<span id="S1.F2.10.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Continuous interactions lead to ever-growing contexts that incur
high memory usage (red line). This motivates the need for compression, yet raises key questions on
<span id="S1.F2.10.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">how to optimize the compressor</span> and
<span id="S1.F2.10.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">reduce its cost</span>. We address these
with <span id="S1.F2.10.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> through a
</span>compression guideline optimization<span id="S1.F2.10.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> and
</span>compressor distillation<span id="S1.F2.10.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium">, effectively
reducing peak tokens (blue line) while preserving essential information.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

The challenge of managing this context is particularly critical in productivity scenarios, such as
email management or workflow automation, where agents must coordinate across heterogeneous
tools (<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">39</a>; <a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">44</a>).
Unlike simpler conversational tasks, these environments demand the preservation of diverse signal
types: factual history, action-outcome relationships, success preconditions, and future decision
cues. Naive strategies like token truncation or generic summarization are insufficient, as they
easily discard these critical details essential for multi-step reasoning. Consequently, effective
compression must balance aggressive reduction with the precise retention of task-relevant state
information.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Existing compression approaches, however, fail to fully address these agent-specific needs.
Dialogue-oriented systems (<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">27</a>) focus on conversational
coherence rather than state tracking, while document-centric
methods (<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">12</a>) assume single-step reasoning where context can
be discarded after use. While recent agent-focused methods attempt to bridge this gap, they face
significant limitations: heuristic-based approaches (<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">7</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">35</a>) are often brittle and narrowly specialized, limiting
their robustness. Meanwhile, model optimization-based
approaches (<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">61</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">36</a>) typically entangle compression with the agent model,
making them difficult to apply directly to proprietary, API-based LLMs, where gradient-based updates
to the underlying model are infeasible.

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

To address these challenges, we introduce <span id="S1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agent
Context Optimization (<span id="S1.p4.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span>)</span>, a unified framework for optimizing the
compression of both environment observations and interaction histories. Distinct from heuristic
approaches that rely on static rules and model optimization-based methods that require model
parameter updates, <span id="S1.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> introduces a
<span id="S1.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">compression guideline optimization</span>
directly in natural language space. This method refines compressor prompts through failure analysis
ensuring that critical environment-specific signals are preserved after compression without altering
the agent model weight (<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref">29</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">52</a>; <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">58</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">8</a>). It makes <span id="S1.p4.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> purely model-agnostic and directly applicable to
agents based on proprietary, API-based LLMs.

</div>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> yields three key advantages
over previous works. First, the guideline optimization enables environment-specific compression
rules to be derived consistently across diverse agentic tasks, overcoming the brittleness of
handcrafted heuristics. Second, by retaining essential information, optimally compressed contexts
not only reduce memory costs but also improve decision quality, allowing smaller models to act more
effectively by mitigating distraction. Third, we validate that these optimized compressors can be
distilled into smaller models, demonstrating that the compression module itself can be deployed with
minimal computational overhead.

</div>

<div id="S1.p6" class="ltx_para">

We validate <span id="S1.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> on three multi-step
agent benchmarks: AppWorld (<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">39</a>),
OfficeBench (<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">44</a>), and Multi-objective
QA (<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">19</a>; <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">61</a>), each
requiring 15+ interaction steps. Our empirical results demonstrate clear advantages of
<span id="S1.p6.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span>: (1) lowers peak token usage of
agents by 26–54% while improving task success compared to existing compression baselines (2) enables
effective distillation of the context compressor into smaller models, preserving 95% of the
teacher’s accuracy, thereby reducing the overhead of the compression (3) allows small LMs to
function more effectively as agents, improving performance by 32% on AppWorld, 20% on OfficeBench,
and 46% on Multi-objective QA by mitigating the distraction of long contexts. Our result highlights
on AppWorld benchmark are in <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure 1</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S1.p7" class="ltx_para">

In summary, our work makes the following contributions:

- <span id="S1.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We propose <span id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agent Context
  Optimization</span> (<span id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span>),
  a framework for optimizing compression of both environment observations and interaction histories,
  tailored to multi-step, long-horizon agentic tasks.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We develop a failure-driven compression guideline optimization. This approach is model-agnostic,
  making it readily applicable to any LLM, including proprietary API-based models, without requiring
  weight updates.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We enable cost-efficient deployment of optimized compressors by distilling them into smaller
  models, preserving over <span id="S1.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">95%</span> of
  the teacher’s performance while reducing the overhead of compression.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We validate <span id="S1.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> on
  AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Multi-objective QA, showing that it reduces peak token usage by
  <span id="S1.I1.i4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">26–54%</span> while improving task
  success over existing compression baselines with LLMs, and enabling small LMs to achieve
  <span id="S1.I1.i4.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">20–46%</span> performance improvements.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Related Works

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Long-horizon LLM agents.

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Large language model (LLM) agents extend pretrained models beyond static single-step reasoning tasks
(e.g., RAG-based QA, math problem solving, or code generation) to interactive decision-making in
dynamic environments (<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">54</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">43</a>; <a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">38</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">23</a>). Unlike chatbots or solvers that return an answer in one
pass, agents must iteratively observe their surroundings, select tools, and execute actions while
revising their plans based on feedback (<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref">34</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">13</a>; <a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">60</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">45</a>; <a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">48</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">3</a>). Recent work highlights the importance of *long-horizon
LLM agents*, which tackle tasks that unfold over dozens to hundreds of steps and require
coordination across multiple applications and tools (<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">18</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">39</a>; <a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">44</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">42</a>). A central challenge in these scenarios lies in
managing the *dynamic long context*, where the agent must retain multi-step interaction histories
and handle diverse observations produced by heterogeneous environments.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Context compression for LLMs.

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Managing this ever-growing context has been a longstanding challenge, and a variety of approaches
have been proposed to compress LLM inputs. Prior works on context compression can be broadly grouped
into three directions: document- or retrieval-based
compression (<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">30</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">21</a>; <a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">49</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">56</a>; <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">61</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">12</a>; <a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">31</a>), dialogue
memory summarization (<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">50</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">22</a>; <a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">41</a>), and
low-level KV cache compression (<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">59</a>). While each line of
research has demonstrated benefits in its respective setting, they remain insufficient for the
dynamic and heterogeneous contexts required by long-horizon agents, where the relevance of
information frequently shifts as the agent progresses.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Beyond general compression, several recent works have explored context compression specifically for
LLM agents (<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">7</a>; <a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">20</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">35</a>; <a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref">57</a>). However,
these approaches either rely on naive prompting or target narrow domains, limiting their broader
applicability. Another related line of works treats context compression as an agent
action (<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">61</a>; <a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">55</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">36</a>), employing reinforcement learning to optimize the model
for both compression and agent action policy. However, such methods inherently update the model to
couple reasoning with compression, typically requiring access to internal weights. In addition,
ReSum (<a href="#bib.bib59" class="ltx_ref">47</a>) shares the motivation of extending long-horizon
agents through summarized contexts, but it optimizes the policy model to better utilize summaries.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">In contrast,</span> we introduce a
universal optimization framework for agent context compression that is applicable to any arbitrary
LLMs. Our framework distinguishes itself by supporting both history and observation compression and
providing a generalizable optimization process for the compression. Since our approach is entirely
<span id="S2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">model-agnostic</span>, it remains
equally effective for both open-source models and proprietary API-based LLMs. A detailed analysis is
provided in <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Appendix C Additional Results ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix C</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Agent Context Optimization (<span id="S3.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span>)

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We present Agent Context Optimization (<span id="S3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span>), a unified framework for optimized history and
observation compression in long-horizon LLM agents. We begin by formulating the agent task and
defining context cost in <a href="#S3.SS1" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="3.1 Problem Formulation ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 3.1</span></a>. Next,
in <a href="#S3.SS2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="3.2 History &amp; Observation Compression with LLMs ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 3.2</span></a>, we introduce generative compression with LLMs
for both history and observation, and formalize the associated optimization objective and its
challenges. We then propose our optimization method
in <a href="#S3.SS3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="3.3 Optimizing Compression Guidelines ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 3.3</span></a>, followed by a distillation that enables smaller
models for compressions to reduce the compression cost
(<a href="#S3.SS4" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="3.4 Distilling Context Compression into Small Models ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 3.4</span></a>).

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Problem Formulation

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Task.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

An agentic task is formulated as a Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP)
$`{\mathcal{E}}=\left\langle{\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{R}}\right\rangle`$
with state space $`{\mathcal{S}}`$, action space $`{\mathcal{A}}`$, and observation space
$`{\mathcal{O}}`$. The transition function $`{\mathcal{T}}(s,a)\rightarrow(s^{\prime},o)`$ is
deterministic, and it is determined by the environment. Specifically, it executes an action
$`a\in{\mathcal{A}}`$ in the environment and returns the next state and observation. The reward
function $`{\mathcal{R}}`$ returns the reward given the terminal state $`s_{T}`$. The terminal state
is arrived when the transition function receives the special action (e.g.,
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_markedasmath ltx_font_typewriter">finish_task</span>).

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

An LLM agent interacts with the environment to get information for making a decision to achieve a
given task $`o_{0}`$ through multiple steps. For each step $`t`$, the LLM $`{\mathcal{M}}`$
generates the action $`a_{t}`$ followed by its reasoning at each
step (<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">54</a>; <a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">43</a>)
given the interaction history $`{\bm{h}}_{t-1}=(o_{0},a_{0},o_{1},a_{1},\cdots,o_{t-1},a_{t-1})`$
and the latest observation $`o_{t}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\vskip-3.61371pt{\mathcal{M}}(o_{t},{\bm{h}}_{t-1};\theta,{\mathcal{P}}_{\sf agent})\mapsto a_{t},\vskip-0.72229pt
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(1)</span> |

where $`\theta`$ refers to the pre-trained parameters of the LLM and $`{\mathcal{P}}_{\sf agent}`$
is the prompt that consists of a general environment description, tools, output format, and few-shot
examples in natural language.

</div>

<figure id="S3.F3" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2510.00615v3/03_method.png" id="S3.F3.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="476" height="100"
alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S3.F3.4" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">Figure 3</span>: </span><span id="S3.F3.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">Compression Guideline Optimization.<span id="S3.F3.5.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Feedback is generated by contrasting successful trajectories (no
compression) with failed ones (with compression). The collected feedback is then used by LLM to
refine the compression guidelines.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Cost function for context.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We assume that the LLM agent’s parameters $`\theta`$ and the task and system prompt
$`{\mathcal{P}}_{\sf agent}`$ are fixed. We define a cost function $`{\mathcal{C}}`$ that measures
the cost of encoding the dynamic context during action generation at each step such as
$`{\mathcal{O}}(n)`$ computational cost of a transformer for decoding given $`n`$ input tokens. The
cost function takes the interaction history $`{\bm{h}}_{t-1}`$, and the latest observation $`o_{t}`$
as input and returns the per-step cost:

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C({\bm{H}})=\sum\nolimits_{t=1}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}({\bm{h}}_{t-1},o_{t}),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(2)</span> |

where $`C`$ is the total cost of completing the task,
$`{\bm{H}}=\{{\bm{h}}_{t-1},o_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}`$ denotes the sequence of history and observation of
each step. Typically, $`C`$ is proportional to the summation of token lengths of action and
observations in each step, $`{\bm{h}}_{t-1}`$ and $`o_{t}`$. While the cost of system prompt is
static, the costs from interaction histories are <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">unbounded</span>, leaving the user with only two options: terminate
the task early or truncate the context heuristically to a maximum length. This raises the central
question: *how can we compress context more effectively than such heuristics?*

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>History & Observation Compression with LLMs

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

To address this challenge, we use an LLM $`f(\cdot;\phi,{\mathcal{P}})`$, parameterized by
pre-trained weights $`\phi`$ and a compression guideline $`{\mathcal{P}}`$, to minimize context cost
defined in <a href="#S3.E2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 2 ‣ Cost function for context. ‣ 3.1 Problem Formulation ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 2</span></a> (e.g., <span id="S3.SS2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">summarize the given interaction history</span>). As
in <a href="#S3.E1" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 1 ‣ Task. ‣ 3.1 Problem Formulation ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 1</span></a>, the LLM receives two inputs at each step: the
interaction history $`{\bm{h}}_{t-1}`$ and the latest observation $`o_{t}`$. This introduces two
options for context compression:

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### History compression.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The interaction history accumulates both environment observations and agent actions. In long-horizon
tasks, this history can grow excessively large. To manage its length, we apply history compression
only when the length exceeds a predefined threshold $`T_{\sf hist}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
{\bm{h}}_{t}^{\prime}=f({\bm{h}}_{t};\phi,{\mathcal{P}}_{\sf hist})\;\;\text{if }|{\bm{h}}_{t}|>T_{\sf hist},\quad{\bm{h}}_{t}\;\;\text{otherwise}.
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(3)</span> |

The compressed history $`{\bm{h}}_{t}^{\prime}`$ replaces the raw history
in <a href="#S3.E1" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 1 ‣ Task. ‣ 3.1 Problem Formulation ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 1</span></a>. This selective compression ensures that the
overhead of invoking the compressor is incurred only when
necessary (<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">35</a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Latest observation compression.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Given an action $`a`$, the environment returns an observation $`o`$ according to the transition
function $`\mathcal{T}(s,a)\rightarrow(s^{\prime},o)`$. We similarly apply observation compression
only when the observation length exceeds a threshold $`T_{\sf obs}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
o_{t}^{\prime}=f(o_{t},{\bm{h}}_{t-1};\phi,{\mathcal{P}}_{\sf obs})\;\;\text{if }|o_{t}|>T_{\sf obs},\quad o_{t}\;\;\text{otherwise}.
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(4)</span> |

This mechanism avoids unnecessary overhead when $`o_{t}`$ is already short, while still reducing
redundant or distracting content in long observations (<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">49</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">7</a>; <a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">20</a>). The
compressed one $`o_{t}^{\prime}`$ replaces the raw one
in <a href="#S3.E1" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 1 ‣ Task. ‣ 3.1 Problem Formulation ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 1</span></a> and is stored in the interaction history
$`{\bm{h}}`$.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

In both cases, the compressor LLM selects information to preserve based on its learned prior
knowledge of importance. However, there is <span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">no guarantee</span> that the salient details required for successful
task completion are retained. The agent context effectively serves as a
<span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">world model of the
environment</span>, encompassing diverse forms of information such as causal relations
(<span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.,</span> email leaves
drafts), evolving states (<span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.,</span> account balance), preconditions
(<span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.,</span> login required),
and task-relevant decision cues (<span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.,</span> due dates). Effective context compression must
therefore accommodate this heterogeneous and dynamic nature of agent context, ensuring that the most
critical signals are preserved for long-horizon reasoning and task success.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Optimization objective.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We optimize the compressor parameters $`\psi\triangleq(\phi,{\mathcal{P}})`$ to maximize task reward
while minimizing context cost. At each step $`t`$, the compressor produces either a compressed
history $`{\bm{h}}_{t}^{\prime}=f_{\sf hist}({\bm{h}}_{t};\psi)`$ or observation
$`o_{t}^{\prime}=f_{\sf obs}(o_{t},{\bm{h}}_{t-1};\psi)`$. Let the compressed context be

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle{\bm{H}}^{\prime}(\psi)\;=\;\{\,{\bm{h}}_{t-1}^{\prime},\,o_{t}^{\prime}\,\}_{t=1}^{T},
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(5)</span> |
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle C({\bm{H}}^{\prime}(\psi))\;=\;\sum\nolimits_{t=1}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}({\bm{h}}_{t-1}^{\prime},o_{t}^{\prime}).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(6)</span> |

With the agent $`{\mathcal{M}}(\cdot;\theta,{\mathcal{P}}_{\sf agent})`$ fixed, the environment
induces a trajectory $`\tau(\psi)`$ and terminal state $`s_{T}(\psi)`$ when the agent conditions on
$`{\bm{H}}^{\prime}(\psi)`$. Our learning objective is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\max_{\psi}\;\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\!\left[\,{\mathcal{R}}\!\big(s_{T}(\psi)\big)\,\right]}_{\text{maximize}}\;-\;\lambda\,\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\!\left[\,C\!\big({\bm{H}}^{\prime}(\psi)\big)\,\right]}_{\text{minimize}},\quad\lambda\geq 0,
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(7)</span> |

where $`\lambda`$ is a multiplier and the expectations are over tasks.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Challenges.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

The optimization objective in <a href="#S3.E7" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 7 ‣ Optimization objective. ‣ 3.2 History &amp; Observation Compression with LLMs ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 7</span></a> is difficult to optimize in practice because
there is no gold supervision for compression, the reward is sparse and only revealed at the end of
the trajectory, and the context cost is defined over discrete quantities, which precludes direct
gradient computation. While these properties naturally motivate reinforcement learning
(RL) (<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref">37</a>), applying RL introduces additional obstacles: (1)
updating the parameters $`\phi`$ of a LLM with RL can be computationally prohibitive, (2)
environment rollouts are extremely expensive since each reward requires multi-step executions of
both agent and compressor, and (3) policy gradient estimates suffer from high variance since
compression quality is only indirectly evaluated through eventual task success.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.3 </span>Optimizing Compression Guidelines

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

To overcome these challenges, we propose to optimize <span id="S3.SS3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">compression guidelines $`{\mathcal{P}}`$</span> (natural language
prompts) for context compression, rather than fine-tuning model parameters $`\phi`$. Trajectories
under compressed contexts provide *dense signals* about the quality of compression. For example, if
the agent fails with compressed context while succeeding without compression, this indicates that
the compressed context may have lost crucial information. Such trajectory-level comparisons yield
richer feedback than scalar rewards (e.g., binary task success).

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

We instantiate this idea as prompt optimization using an LLM as the optimizer, where the natural
language prompt $`{\mathcal{P}}`$ is refined via feedback expressed in natural
language (<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">52</a>; <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">58</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref">15</a>; <a href="#bib.bib61" class="ltx_ref">4</a>). We
introduce <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">compression guideline
optimization</span> based on *contrastive task feedback*.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

On the training set $`{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf train}`$, we run the LLM agent both without and with
context compression to obtain baseline context $`{\bm{H}}`$ and compressed context
$`{\bm{H}}^{\prime}`$. We collect tasks where the agent succeeds with $`{\bm{H}}`$ but fails with
$`{\bm{H}}^{\prime}`$, forming a contrastive subset $`{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf cont}`$. For each task in
$`{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf cont}`$, we query a optimizer LLM with the context before and after compression
to obtain natural language feedback:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle\text{Feedback}=\text{LLM}(\text{Feedback Instruction},{\bm{H}},{\bm{H}}^{\prime}).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(8)</span> |

This feedback serves as a natural language gradient (<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">58</a>),
indicating how the compression guideline $`{\mathcal{P}}`$ should be refined. We then aggregate
feedback from multiple trajectories:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)}=\text{LLM}(\text{Update Inst.},{\mathcal{P}}^{(0)},\textstyle\|_{i=1}^{n}\text{Feedback}_{i}),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(9)</span> |

where $`\|`$ is concatenation of feedbacks from each task, which corresponds to a batch optimization
step in textual gradient descent (<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">58</a>). We also generate
multiple candidate prompts $`\{{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{(1)}\}`$, evaluate them on
$`{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf cont}`$, and select the best-performing one. We refer this process as *utility
maximization* step <span id="S3.SS3.p3.3.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;">
$`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span> as it primarily maximizes the first term (task reward)
of <a href="#S3.E7" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 7 ‣ Optimization objective. ‣ 3.2 History &amp; Observation Compression with LLMs ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 7</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para">

However, optimizing only for reward may neglect the context cost (second term
in <a href="#S3.E7" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 7 ‣ Optimization objective. ‣ 3.2 History &amp; Observation Compression with LLMs ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 7</span></a>). To address this, motivated by alternating
optimization, we perform a second iteration that conditions only on successful task with compressed
context, asking the LLM to generate feedback about which information was actually used during
execution. This refines $`{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}^{(2)}`$, encouraging shorter
yet sufficient contexts. We refer this additional process as *compression maximization* step
<span id="S3.SS3.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#4B0082;">
$`\overline{\underline{\textsc{co}}}`$</span> as it minimizes the second term (context cost)
of <a href="#S3.E7" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 7 ‣ Optimization objective. ‣ 3.2 History &amp; Observation Compression with LLMs ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 7</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p5" class="ltx_para">

We illustrate overall process in <a href="#S3.F3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Figure 3 ‣ Task. ‣ 3.1 Problem Formulation ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure 3</span></a>. Algorithm <a href="#alg1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Algorithm 1 ‣ Compressed observations. ‣ Appendix D Qualitative Examples ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> and prompts are
in <a href="#A2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Appendix B Experimental Setup Details ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix B</span></a>.

</div>

class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Appworld</span> benchmark (test-normal). Each block reports accuracy
(task goal completion score), steps, peak input tokens (<span
class="math inline">10<sup>3</sup></span>), and dependency (<span
class="math inline">10<sup>6</sup></span>) for agents. Best results in each column are highlighted
in bold. Rows in blue background indicate the results from <span id="S3.T1.10.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="--ltx-bg-color:#E6F5FF;">ours</span>. <span id="S3.T1.10.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> consistently improves accuracy while reducing peak
tokens and dependency, with <span id="S3.T1.10.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> <span id="S3.T1.10.5" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;"> <span
class="math inline">$\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}$</span></span> <span id="S3.T1.10.6"
class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#4B0082;"> <span
class="math inline">$\overline{\underline{\textsc{co}}}$</span></span> achieving the best overall
performance.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.4 </span>Distilling Context Compression into Small Models

<div id="S3.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

While compression guideline optimization enables effective compression, repeatedly invoking the
large LLM for compression adds substantial overhead. To reduce this cost, we
<span id="S3.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">distill the compressor into a smaller
model</span>. The teacher with optimized guideline $`{\mathcal{P}}^{*}`$ (parameters
$`\phi_{\sf T}`$) generates compressed outputs $`{\bm{y}}`$ from input $`{\bm{x}}`$, which supervise
the student (parameters $`\phi_{\sf S}`$). We train the student with a cross-entropy
objective (<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">17</a>) with input-output pair
$`({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})`$, where $`({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})=({\bm{h}}_{t},{\bm{h}}_{t}^{\prime})`$
for <a href="#S3.E3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 3 ‣ History compression. ‣ 3.2 History &amp; Observation Compression with LLMs ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 3</span></a> or
$`({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})=(({\bm{h}}_{t-1},o_{t}),o_{t}^{\prime})`$
for <a href="#S3.E4" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 4 ‣ Latest observation compression. ‣ 3.2 History &amp; Observation Compression with LLMs ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 4</span></a>:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\min_{\phi_{\sf S}}\;\mathbb{E}_{({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\sim\mathcal{D}^{+}_{\sf train}}\Bigg[-\sum_{n=1}^{L_{\bm{y}}}\log f({\bm{y}}_{n}\mid{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}_{<n};\phi_{\sf S},{\mathcal{P}}^{*})\Bigg],
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(10)</span> |

where $`\mathcal{D}^{+}_{\sf train}`$ denotes tasks where the teacher succeeds with compressed
context.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

Once trained, the student replaces the teacher during inference, decoupling decision making from
compression. This two-stage pipeline, guideline optimization then distillation, achieves effective
compression with a much smaller model ($`|\phi_{\sf T}|\gg|\phi_{\sf S}|`$):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f(\cdot;\phi_{\sf T},{\mathcal{P}})\xrightarrow{\;\text{prompt optim.}\;}f(\cdot;\phi_{\sf T},{\mathcal{P}}^{*})\xrightarrow{\;\text{distill}\;}f(\cdot;\phi_{\sf S},{\mathcal{P}}^{*}).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(11)</span> |

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Experiments

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate <span id="S4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> on three
challenging benchmarks that require multi-step interactions across diverse domains. Our experiments
are designed to address the following key questions:

- <span id="S4.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  How well does <span id="S4.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> improve
  token efficiency while preserving performance?
  (<a href="#S4.SS2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
  title="4.2 Overall performance and token efficiency ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 4.2</span></a>)

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Does distilling the compressor reduce its size while maintaining agent performance?
  (<a href="#S4.SS3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
  title="4.3 Compressor distillation ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 4.3</span></a>)

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Can <span id="S4.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> help small,
  distilled LM agents perform better under long contexts?
  (<a href="#S4.SS4" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
  title="4.4 Acon for distilled small agents ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 4.4</span></a>)

  </div>

</div>

trade-offs.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F4.5" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">Figure 4</span>: </span><span id="S4.F4.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">Results of distilled compressors on history compression<span id="S4.F4.6.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> with gpt-4.1 as the agent. Grey dotted lines denote performance
using the gpt-4.1 teacher compressor. Student models (Qwen3-14B, Qwen3-8B, Phi-4) are distilled from
gpt-4.1 compressor using the optimized compression guideline after <span id="S4.F4.6.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;"> <span
class="math inline">$\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}$</span></span> step, and evaluated across
all benchmarks. We also include gpt-4.1-mini without distillation, showing that even a small model
can serve as an effective compressor without additional training.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Experimental Setup

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Benchmarks & Metrics.

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We focus on long-horizon agentic task benchmarks that require $`10+`$ interaction steps on average:
<span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1)
AppWorld</span> (<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">39</a>): Main benchmark with 9 simulated apps
(e.g., Venmo, Spotify, SimpleNote) and $`\sim`$<!-- -->100 simulated users. Performance is measured
by task completion score. <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2)
OfficeBench</span> (<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">44</a>): Productivity tasks across 6 apps
(e.g., Word, Excel, Email), operating on simulated documents. Performance is measured by
benchmark-defined accuracy functions. <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(3) 8-objective
QA</span> (<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">19</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">61</a>): QA benchmark where agents interact with a search tool
to answer 8 questions and output a consolidated answer set. Performance is the average of Exact
Match (EM) and F1 scores across 8 questions.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

In addition to task-specific performance metrics, we report three token efficiency metrics following
prior work (<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">59</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">61</a>): <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1) Steps:</span> The average number of interaction steps per task.
<span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2) Peak Tokens:</span> The maximum
context length encountered across all steps. <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(3) Dependency:</span> The cumulative dependency of each generated
action on prior tokens, measuring how much generation relies on the context history. Full details
are provided in the <a href="#A2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Appendix B Experimental Setup Details ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix B</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Throughout all experiments, we use ReAct agent (<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">54</a>). For
the detailed tool format, we follow the convention of each benchmark.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Baselines.

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1) No Compression:</span> full
uncompressed context. <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2)
FIFO:</span> keep the most recent $`k`$ interactions, discarding earlier
ones (<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">53</a>). <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(3) Retrieval:</span> select $`k`$ past interactions most similar to
the current query via embedding search (<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">50</a>).
<span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(4) LLMLingua:</span> extractive
compression with an encoder-only LM (<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">11</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">28</a>). <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(5) Prompting:</span> naive baseline using a general compression
instruction (<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">35</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">20</a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Our Methods.

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate two versions of <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span>. <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1) <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.2.2" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;"> $`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span></span> utilizes an
*optimized guideline* for context compression after utility maximization step.
<span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2)
<span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.4.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.4.2" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;"> $`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span>
<span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.4.3" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#4B0082;">
$`\overline{\underline{\textsc{co}}}`$</span></span> applies compression
maximization <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#4B0082;">
$`\overline{\underline{\textsc{co}}}`$</span> after utility
maximization <span id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;">
$`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span>, aiming for shorter but informative compression. We
apply only single step per each step for experiments. Additional analysis on the number of steps is
in <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Appendix C Additional Results ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix C</span></a>.

</div>

<figure id="S4.F5" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F5.5" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">Figure 5</span>: </span><span id="S4.F5.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">Performance-efficiency trade-off of the Qwen3-14B agent<span id="S4.F5.6.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> distilled from gpt-4.1 trajectories. For distilled compressors, we
use the same distillation setting as in <a href="#S4.F4" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure 4</span></a>. Compared to the baseline without compression, our
framework <span id="S4.F5.6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> provides compressed
trajectories combined with a distilled compressor, enabling the distilled agent to achieve
consistently higher accuracy while requiring substantially fewer peak input tokens across all
benchmarks.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<figure id="S4.F6" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S4.F6.fig1" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S4.F6.fig2" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S4.F6.fig3" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F6.4" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">Figure 6</span>: </span><span id="S4.F6.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">Ablation studies on thresholds for compression<span id="S4.F6.5.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> on AppWorld with gpt-4.1. (1) the number of compressions
(compression frequency) for each length of task trajectories (task steps). (2) the performance
comparison for each threshold setting.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>Overall performance and token efficiency

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

In <a href="#S3.T1" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 3.3 Optimizing Compression Guidelines ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table 1</span></a>
and <a href="#S4.T2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table 2</span></a>, we first evaluate <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> using on
gpt-4.1 (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">24</a>) for both agent and compressor, which already
achieves strong results on benchmarks.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

For history compression, as shown in <a href="#S3.T1" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 3.3 Optimizing Compression Guidelines ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table 1</span></a>, on AppWorld, <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon<span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_upright"> reduces peak tokens by over 25%</span></span> while preserving
the accuracy of the no compression upper bound, outperforming all baselines that suffer severe
degradation on medium and hard tasks spanning longer steps. On OfficeBench
(<a href="#S4.T2.st1" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Table 2(a) ‣ Table 2 ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2(a)</span></a>), <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> lowers peak context size by nearly 30% while
maintaining accuracy above 74%. On 8-objective QA
(<a href="#S4.T2.st2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Table 2(b) ‣ Table 2 ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2(b)</span></a>), <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> even surpasses the no compression baseline in EM/F1
while reducing peak tokens and dependency by 54.5% and 61.5%, respectively. For observation
compression, <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> consistently
outperforms all baselines confirming that compression guideline optimization is effective for
compressing not only history but also raw observations.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Applying only the utility maximization step (<span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;"> $`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span>) improves performance
while reducing token cost across all benchmarks, whereas the compression maximization step
(<span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#4B0082;">
$`\overline{\underline{\textsc{co}}}`$</span>) further lowers token cost but may slightly hurt
accuracy depending on the environment. This trade-off suggests a practical guideline for choosing
between the two variants. In verbose and noisy environments such as AppWorld, where observations
contain redundant API outputs and distractors, <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;"> $`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span>
<span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#4B0082;">
$`\overline{\underline{\textsc{co}}}`$</span> is often preferable because aggressive pruning can
improve both efficiency and focus. In contrast, in high-fidelity information-seeking tasks such as
OfficeBench and 8-objective QA, <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;"> $`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span> is generally safer
because over-compression may remove subtle facts needed for final decisions. Therefore,
<span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.6" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;">
$`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span> <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.7" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#4B0082;"> $`\overline{\underline{\textsc{co}}}`$</span> is recommended when
observations contain substantial redundancy, while <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.8" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;"> $`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span> is preferable when
precise fact retention is critical.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.3 </span>Compressor distillation

<div id="S4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We distill the compressor with optimized guidelines after <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-fg-color:#D2691E;"> $`\overline{\underline{\textsc{ut}}}`$</span> step into smaller
models such as Qwen3-14B, Qwen3-8B (<a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">51</a>), and
Phi-4 (<a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">1</a>) using
LoRA (<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">10</a>). As shown
in <a href="#S4.F4" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure 4</span></a>, the <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">distilled compressors retain over 95% of the performance of the
gpt-4.1 teacher</span> (indicated by the grey dotted line) while reducing computational overhead. We
also observe that gpt-4.1-mini, even without any distillation, can serve as an effective lightweight
compressor on OfficeBench and QA. This indicates that small models can reliably replace large
LLM-based compressors when equipped with optimized guidelines. These results confirm that small
models are sufficient for compression, enabling the expensive LLM to be only reserved for the agent.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.4 </span><span id="S4.SS4.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> for distilled small agents

<div id="S4.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

We examine whether <span id="S4.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> also
benefits smaller LLM agents, which are particularly vulnerable to long-horizon inefficiency. Without
compression, models such as Qwen3-14B often fail on medium and hard tasks due to distracting
context. As shown in <a href="#S4.F5" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Figure 5 ‣ Our Methods. ‣ 4.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure 5</span></a>, <span id="S4.SS4.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> substantially improves their performance: on
AppWorld, Qwen3-14B achieves a <span id="S4.SS4.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">32.4%
relative improvement</span> (from 25.6% to 33.9%), and on 8-objective QA, it shows a
<span id="S4.SS4.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">45.6% gain</span> (from 0.158 to 0.23 EM).
These results demonstrate that <span id="S4.SS4.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> acts as an equalizer, enabling smaller agents with
concise but informative contexts to approach the performance of larger models.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS5" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.5 </span>Analysis

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Compression threshold: moderate value yields the best trade-off.

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

In <a href="#S4.F6" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Figure 6 ‣ Our Methods. ‣ 4.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure 6</span></a>, we provide ablations on threshold for compression
in <a href="#S3.E3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 3 ‣ History compression. ‣ 3.2 History &amp; Observation Compression with LLMs ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 3</span></a>
and <a href="#S3.E4" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Equation 4 ‣ Latest observation compression. ‣ 3.2 History &amp; Observation Compression with LLMs ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Equation 4</span></a>. Results show that smaller thresholds reduce
tokens but incur more frequent compression calls and degrade accuracy, while larger thresholds
preserve accuracy with higher cost. Moderate values (4096 for history, 1024 for observation) provide
the best trade-off, maintaining accuracy close to no compression while still reducing peak tokens
substantially.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Prompt optimizer: <span id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">o3</span> + contrastive feedback works best.

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We analyze how the choice of optimizer and the use of contrastive feedback affect compression
guideline quality. As shown in <a href="#S4.T3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Table 3 ‣ Prompt optimizer: o3 + contrastive feedback works best. ‣ 4.5 Analysis ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table 3</span></a>, the default <span id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">o3</span> with contrastive feedback yields the best
performance, while removing contrastive feedback (only using failed trajectories) or switching to
other models results in lower accuracy. Although <span id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">o3</span> shows the best performance, we also demonstrate that
the optimizer model can be replaced to weaker models such as gpt-4.1, showing it still yields
sufficiently fine guideline compared to the baseline guideline.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Optimization cost: practical and lightweight.

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

One might assume that leveraging a reasoning model like <span id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">o3</span> would be cost-prohibitive. However, our guideline
optimization step is remarkably lightweight, demanding *less than \$2 per benchmark*. This can be
negligible compared to the expense of trajectory rollouts on the training dataset. For instance,
utilizing gpt-4.1 on AppWorld train set requires a total rollout cost of approximately \$20.
Furthermore, even this data collection cost is substantially lower than that of RL-based methods
such as GRPO (<a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref">32</a>), which requires extensive rollout for
advantage estimation. Detailed API cost computation and breakdowns are provided
in <a href="#A2.SS2" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="B.2 Evaluation Metrics ‣ Appendix B Experimental Setup Details ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section B.2</span></a> and <a href="#A3.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.2 Detailed results and analyses ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.2</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### API cost analysis of compressors across different models.

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

While using a compressor reduces context length, it incurs additional computational costs beyond the
agent. In <a href="#S3.SS4" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="3.4 Distilling Context Compression into Small Models ‣ 3 Agent Context Optimization (Acon) ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section 3.4</span></a>, we proposed a distillation strategy to mitigate
this overhead. To quantify the cost efficiency, we analyze the expenses using API pricing as a
proxy. With gpt-4.1 as the baseline compressor, the cost remains significant at \$0.045 per example.
Switching to gpt-4.1-mini reduces the compression cost to \$0.014, achieving a 69.2% reduction.
However, the most substantial gain is observed with distilled Qwen3-14B, where the cost decreases to
\$0.0004. This represents a 99.1% reduction compared to the teacher model, effectively minimizing
the cost burden of context compression.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Practical efficiency trade-off.

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para">

We further quantify the practical overhead of generative compression on AppWorld. For API cost, we
estimate the end-to-end cost of both the agent and compressor, excluding input caching. For latency,
we run the agent and compressor locally on a single A100 GPU rather than using API endpoints, which
avoids confounding factors such as network overhead and server-side queueing. As shown
in <a href="#S4.T4" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Table 4 ‣ Practical efficiency trade-off. ‣ 4.5 Analysis ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table 4</span></a>, ACON reduces end-to-end API cost by compressing the
context of agent, but introduces additional wall-clock latency due to the extra compressor call.

</div>

<figure id="S4.T4" class="ltx_table">
<div id="S4.T4.8" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_center ltx_transformed_outer"
style="width:293.3pt;height:71.4pt;vertical-align:-32.4pt;">

<div id="S4.SS5.SSS0.Px5.p2" class="ltx_para">

We include more experimental results including experiments with different agent models, additional
ablation studies, case study, and qualitative examples of context compression
in <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Appendix C Additional Results ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix C</span></a> and <a href="#A4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix D Qualitative Examples ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Conclusion

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">

We presented <span id="S5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agent Context Optimization
(<span id="S5.p1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span>)</span>, a unified framework
that systematically compresses both interaction histories and environment observations for
long-horizon LLM agents. Unlike prior work that relies on naive prompting or narrow domains,
<span id="S5.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> introduces compression
guideline optimization in natural language space, enabling adaptive and model-agnostic compression.
Experiments on AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Multi-objective QA show that ACON reduces peak tokens by
26–54% while improving task success over existing compression baselines, with small degradation
relative to full-context baseline. Beyond memory efficiency, we demonstrate that optimized
compressors can be distilled into smaller models, substantially lowering overhead without
sacrificing performance. Moreover, by supplying concise yet informative contexts,
<span id="S5.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> allows small agents such as
Qwen3-14B to approach the performance of much larger models. Overall, our findings highlight that
<span id="S5.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Acon</span> lays a foundation for more
general, memory-efficient, and deployable long-horizon LLM agents.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Limitations and Future Work.

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

While ACON effectively reduces context costs, a few limitations remain. First, our empirical
evaluation primarily focuses on GPT models due to resource constraints. Second, like many context
management frameworks, the compression process itself introduces computational overhead and
increased latency. We include more detailed discussion on limitations and future work
in <a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref ltx_refmacro_autoref"
title="Appendix A Limitations &amp; Future Works ‣ Acon: Optimizing Context Compression for Long-horizon LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix A</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="Sx1" class="section ltx_section">

### Impact Statement

<div id="Sx1.p1" class="ltx_para">

This paper presents work whose goal is to advance the field of Large Language Models, specifically
by addressing the computational costs of long-horizon autonomous agents. By optimizing context
compression and enabling effective distillation into smaller models, our work primarily contributes
to making advanced agentic capabilities more resource-efficient and accessible. This has positive
implications for reducing the computational footprint of AI systems and lowering barriers to entry
for researchers with limited resources. We do not foresee specific negative societal consequences
beyond the general considerations required when deploying autonomous systems.

</div>

</div>
